                      HOUSE JOURNAL
            EIGHTY-NINTH LEGISLATURE, REGULAR SESSION

                                 PROCEEDINGS

             SIXTY-NINTH DAY — WEDNESDAY, MAY 21, 2025
    The house met at 11:15 a.m. and was called to order by the speaker pro
tempore.
    The roll of the house was called and a quorum was announced present
(Recordi3114).

     Absent, Excused — Jones, V.
                      LEAVES OF ABSENCE GRANTED
    On motion of Representative Patterson and by unanimous consent, all
members who were granted leaves of absence on the previous legislative day
were granted leaves for this legislative day.
                              RULES SUSPENDED
     Representative Patterson moved to suspend all necessary rules to take up
and consider at this time, on third reading and final passage, the bills on the local,
consent, and resolutions calendar which were considered on the previous
legislative day.
     The motion prevailed.
5440             89th LEGISLATURE — REGULAR SESSION

                     MOTION FOR ONE RECORD VOTE
      On motion of Representative Patterson and by unanimous consent, the house
 agreed to use the first record vote taken for all those bills on the local, consent,
 and resolutions calendar that require a record vote on third reading and final
 passage, with the understanding that a member may record an individual vote on
 any bill with the journal clerk.

                          SB 1253 ON THIRD READING
                             (C. Bell - House Sponsor)
     SB 1253, A bill to be entitled An Act relating to the provision by a political
 subdivision of credits against impact fees to builders and developers for certain
 water conservation and reuse projects.
 Amendment No. 1
       Representative Romero offered the following amendment to SBi1253:
      Amend SBi1253 (house committee report) on third reading as follows:
      (1)iiOn page 2, between lines 7 and 8, insert the following:
      SECTION 2.iiSection 8843.152, Special District Local Laws Code, is
 amended to read as follows:
      Sec.i8843.152. SERVICE CONNECTION FEE AND PRODUCTION FEE.
 (a) This section does not apply to a water utility that has surface water as its sole
 source of water.
      (b)iiThe district may levy and collect a water utility service connection fee
 not to exceed $1,000 for each new water service connection made after
 September 1, 2013.
      (c)iiThe district may assess a production fee as provided by Section 36.205,
 Water Code. Subject to Subsection (d), a fee assessed under this subsection may
 not exceed 30 cents per 1,000 gallons.
      (d)iiBeginning January 1, 2026, and continuing on January 1 of each
 subsequent year, the maximum amount of the fee described by Subsection (c)
 increases by five percent from the previous year ’s maximum amount.
Wednesday, May 21, 2025         HOUSE JOURNAL — 69th Day                             5453

       (e)iiThe district may provide a credit against a service connection fee
 otherwise assessed to a water utility that is also assessed a production fee under
 this section for the construction, contribution, or dedication of an eligible facility,
 system, or product that results in water reuse, conservation, or savings as
 described under Section 395.0231(b), Local Government Code.
       SECTIONi3.iiSection 8843.104, Special District Local Laws Code, is
 repealed.
       (2)iiOn page 2, line 8, strike "SECTION 2" and substitute "SECTION 4".
      Amendment No. 1 was adopted.
                 STATEMENT BY REPRESENTATIVE ISAAC
       The Romero Amendment (Amendment No.i1) to SBi1253 was rammed into
 legislation during third reading without debate or discussion, without public
 notice, and without any reference to the Hays Trinity Groundwater Conservation
 District (HTGCD) in the underlying bill text or in statements on the house floor.
 This is a clear violation of the house ’s long-standing rules on germaneness, and it
 is a slap in the face to every Texan who expects transparency and representation
 in their government. It clearly violates the tradition of the house to allow
 members to represent their districts by not filing bills or amendments that solely
 affect another member ’s district. I would have voted no on this amendment if the
 language and intent had been properly presented to the house. This amendment is
 not about conservation. It is about control, and it is the product of a carefully
 orchestrated campaign driven by special interests, political opportunism, and
 cronyism, not by the will of the people of House District 73 or Hays County.
       What the Romero Amendment would do:
       This amendment is nearly identical to SBi2660, which failed to move
 through the house due to overwhelming local opposition. It would:
           (1)iiAuthorize a "connection fee" of up to $1,000 per water line.
           (2)iiImpose massive new groundwater production fees, with automatic
 5ipercent annual increases in perpetuity.
           (3)iiAllow metering of private wells, including those used for
 agriculture.
           (4)iiAllow HTGCD staff to enter private property without landowner
 consent.
       The issues:
           (1)iiFee Increases Exceed Legal Caps: The amendment allows
 groundwater production fees far above what current law permits and includes an
 automatic 5 percent annual increase not authorized in statute.
           (2)iiNew Water Connection Fee: A $1,000 per-connection charge is
 described as a possible unconstitutional tax since it ’s not contemplated under
 current law and would allow this single district the authority to "double dip" on
 taxation and all done without any notification or notice as constitutionally
 required. Chapter 36 does not contemplate such "double dipping" unless you
 acknowledge the "connection fee" is a "tax." If it is a tax, you have constitutional
 notice/posting issues that have not been complied with. Neither Barton
5454            89th LEGISLATURE — REGULAR SESSION

 Springs-Edwards Aquifer Conservation District (BSEACD) nor Comal Trinity
 GCD have both a "production fee" and a "connection fee" like this legislation
 will grant the HTGCD.
           (3)iiLoss of Well Exemptions: The amendment repeals protections for
 private and agricultural wells, implying that meters could now be required and
 permits may be needed––even though local rules currently exempt these wells.
 Creating a property value crisis and a conflict with long-standing right of capture.
           (4)iiAccess to Private Property: Repealing language in HTGCD ’s
 governing statute would give the district the right to enter private property for
 well inspections without landowner consent, as Chapter 36 of the Water Code
 would then apply.
           (5)iiLegal and Practical Gaps: Finally, the amendment adds a new
 Subsection (e) that is very problematic, in its attempt to make this language
 germane to the bill, it adds vague, permissive language that has never been
 assigned to groundwater districts, nor was it vetted for this particular district at
 any juncture of the 89th Session.
      These are radical powers for a water board. And they are being handed out
 through a legislative backdoor by a member from Fort Worth with no connection
 to Hays County.
      This amendment represents a direct assault on private property rights, local
 control, and the rule of law. It was never vetted, never debated, and never
 requested by the citizens of Hays County. The third reading amendment
 purposefully snuck onto other legislation without debate, public notice, or
 mention of Hays Trinity Groundwater Conservation District (HTGCD) in the
 underlying bill text or in statements on the house floor violates the spirit and the
 integrity of the house. It disrespects local control, undermines voter confidence,
 and sets a dangerous precedent for lawmaking in Texas.
      SB 1253, as amended, was passed by (Record 3126): 112 Yeas, 28 Nays, 2
 Present, not voting. (A motion to reconsider failed later today by Record
 No.i3184.)
      Yeas — Alders; Allen; Anchía; Bell, C.; Bell, K.; Bernal; Bhojani; Bonnen;

 Collier; Cortez; Craddick; Cunningham; Curry; Darby; Davis, A.; Davis, Y.;

                      CSSB 1405 ON SECOND READING
                             (Ashby - House Sponsor)
      CSSB 1405, A bill to be entitled An Act relating to increasing access to and
 reducing taxation of Internet services.
      CSSB 1405 was read second time on Mayi20 and was postponed until
 10ia.m. today.
 Amendment No. 1
      On behalf of Representative Craddick, Representative Curry offered the
 following amendment to CSSBi1405:
      Amend CSSBi1405 (house committee report) as follows:
      (1)iiOn page 11, line 12, between the period and "The", insert "(a)".
      (2)iiOn page 11, between lines 16 and 17, insert the following:
      (b)iiSection 151.3186, Tax Code, is repealed effective January 1, 2027.
      (3)iiOn page 11, line 17, strike "The" and substitute "(a) Except as provided
 by Subsection (b) of this section, the".
      (4)iiOn page 11, between lines 22 and 23, insert the following:
      (b)iiThe repeal of Section 151.3186, Tax Code, by this Act does not affect a
 refund:
           (1)iito which a taxpayer became entitled before the effective date of the
 repeal; and
           (2)iifor which a taxpayer timely filed a claim with the comptroller of
 public accounts on or before March 31, 2027.
Wednesday, May 21, 2025        HOUSE JOURNAL — 69th Day                           5485

       (5)iiOn page 11, line 26, strike "This" and substitute "Except as otherwise
 provided by this Act, this".
       (6)iiAdd the following appropriately numbered SECTION to the bill and
 renumber the SECTIONS of the bill accordingly:
       SECTIONi____.iiEffective January 1, 2028, Subchapter H, Chapter 151,
 Tax Code, is amended by adding Section 151.3187 to read as follows:
       Sec.i151.3187.iiPROPERTY USED IN CABLE TELEVISION, INTERNET
 ACCESS, OR TELECOMMUNICATIONS SERVICES. (a) In this section,
 "provider" means a provider of cable television service, Internet access service,
 or telecommunications services.
       (b)iiNotwithstanding Sections 151.051 and 151.101 and except as provided
 by Subsection (c), the rate of the tax imposed under this chapter on the sale,
 lease, or rental or storage, use, or other consumption of tangible personal property
 is four percent if:
            (1)iithe property is sold, leased, or rented to or stored, used, or
 consumed by a provider or a subsidiary of a provider; and
            (2)iithe property is directly used or consumed by the provider or
 subsidiary described by Subdivision (1) in or during:
                 (A)iithe distribution of cable television service;
                 (B)iithe provision of Internet access service; or
                 (C)iithe transmission, conveyance, routing, or reception of
 telecommunications services.
       (c)iiThis section does not apply to:
            (1)iithe sale, lease, or rental or storage, use, or other consumption of
 tangible personal property directly used or consumed in or during the provision,
 creation, or production of a data processing service or information service; or
            (2)iia tax authorized or imposed under Subtitle C, Title 3.
       (d)iiThe comptroller shall adopt rules to implement this section, including
 rules providing for a partial refund of any amount of tax imposed at the rate
 provided by Subsection (b) but collected at the rate provided by Section
 151.051(b).
      Amendment No. 1 was adopted.

                          MAJOR STATE CALENDAR
                              SENATE BILLS
                            SECOND READING
      The following bills were laid before the house and read second time:
                      SB 31 ON SECOND READING
         (Geren, Johnson, Howard, Hull, Orr, et al. - House Sponsors)
      SB 31, A bill to be entitled An Act relating to exceptions to otherwise
 prohibited abortions based on a physician ’s reasonable medical judgment.
      (Canales, Dorazio, Hefner, and Louderback now present)
                                SB 31 - REMARKS
 REPRESENTATIVE GEREN: My layout ’s going to be a little bit longer than I
 normally do, so please let me get through it. If you have questions, I ’d like to wait
 and answer them after that. The Life of the Mother Act has brought together all
 major pro-life groups, the doctors, the hospitals, Republicans, and Democrats.
 People on both sides of the abortion issue have agreed to this bill. These groups
 include the Texas Catholic Conference of Bishops, the Texas Alliance for Life,
 Texans for Life Committee, Texas Right to Life, the Human Coalition, the Texas
 Medical Association, Texas Hospital Association, Texas Civil Justice League,
 and Texas Campaign for Mothers. This bill passed out of the senate 31-0. As you
 know, these groups don t’ always see eye to eye, but in this case, they worked
 together to ensure pregnant women who have pregnancy complications get
 appropriate and timely care. And why is that? It ’s simple. We do not want women
 to die from medical emergencies during their pregnancy. We don t’ want women ’s
 lives to be destroyed because their bodies have been seriously impaired by
 medical emergencies during their pregnancies. We know women have died after
 care was delayed or denied. We know women have left Texas for lifesaving care.
 We know women ’s bodies have been horribly injured because doctors and
 hospitals are afraid to provide abortions that could save their bodies. Doctors and
 hospitals need the clarity SBi31 will put into the law to be able to save women s’
 lives and protect them from substantial impairments to their bodily functions. On
 all those things we agree. Senator Hughes asked the medical board to adopt rules
 clarifying the law. The Texas Supreme Court also asked the medical board to
 write these rules. When the board finally acted, their rules were inefficient. That
 leaves it up to us as a legislature.
       SBi31 has one goal––to change the behavior of medical professionals by
 providing laws that are clear, consistent, fair, and understandable. Doctors and
 hospitals agree SBi31 will achieve this goal. During this layout, I want to clearly
 state what this bill does and what it does not do. Let ’s start with the five major
 things it does. It provides an exception to the abortion laws when the mother has
 a life-threatening physical condition which could lead to death or threat to one of
 her major bodily functions. It s’ a two-part test which starts with a physician
 determining whether the life of the mom is in a life-threatening physical
 condition. This clarification clarifies the legislative intent everyone thought we
5494             89th LEGISLATURE — REGULAR SESSION

 had when we passed the law several years ago. It makes clear that doctors are not
 required to withhold or alter lifesaving treatment for cancer or other conditions if
 doing so would jeopardize the life or major bodily functions, including the future
 fertility of a pregnant woman. It expressly states in current law the
 life-threatening condition does not have to be imminent. For example, in the case
 of pre-viable premature rupture of membranes––PPROM––will result in the mom
 developing an infection known as sepsis. Sepsis can cause death or seriously
 affect the mom ’s bodily functions. The bill expressly states that the physician
 does not need to wait for the infection to develop before intervening. It provides
 that all parties––doctors, nurses, pharmacists, technicians, and lawyers involved
 in making a medical determination––will not be sued for aiding or abetting an
 abortion. It requires doctors to have continued medical education regarding
 abortion laws and provides continuing legal education for lawyers who advise
 and represent medical professionals in the area of the law. Now, I want to address
 what is not in this bill. This bill does not address rape or incest. It does not
 address fatal fetal abnormalities. And this is not a choice bill. This bill addresses
 the life and health of the mother. Period. That ’s it.
       There are two other issues I would like to further clarify during this layout.
 The first one is called the 1925 law. One of four abortion laws that are amended
 by SBi31 is what has commonly been referred to as the 1925 law. This was the
 original statute banning abortions in Texas. When the Supreme Court made
 abortion legal under Roe v. Wade, the 1925 law was implied to be repealed.
 When Roe v. Wade was overturned in Dobbs in 2022, the reason for the implied
 repeal went away. There is currently a federal case pending which addresses the
 issue of whether the 1925 statute has been revived or not. To this date, the
 legislature has spoken three times, saying it believes the law is allowed.
 Ultimately, the court will make that determination. In the meantime, the provision
 in SBi31 states that the bill is neutral as to whether the 1925 statute has been
 revived or not. By passing this bill, the bill is not taking a position on the revival
 of the statute one way or another. We commonly use provisions like this when
 there is pending litigation.
       The second issue is whether the bill will permit the prosecution of a
 pregnant woman who gets an abortion. The unequivocal answer is no. Our state
 has never prosecuted women who get an abortion, and nothing in SBi31 changes
 that. I want to thank my fellow joint authors, co-sponsors, and stakeholders for
 working with me on this important piece of legislation. Once again, I would like
 to read the people who have worked with us to put this bill together: the Texas
 Catholic Conference of Bishops, the Texas Alliance for Life, Texans for Life
 Committee, Texas Right to Life, the Human Coalition, Texas Medical
 Association, Texas Hospital Association, the Texas Civil Justice League, and the
 Texas Campaign for Mothers.
 REPRESENTATIVE SCHATZLINE: I just have a few clarifying questions just
 so I can understand it. What is the purpose of Section 170A.0021?
 GEREN: I ’m sorry, Mr. Schatzline. Which section?
 SCHATZLINE: 170A.0021. It ’s in Section 4 on page three of the bill.
Wednesday, May 21, 2025         HOUSE JOURNAL — 69th Day                            5495

 GEREN: Yes. I have it here. This section was moved from a previous section
 already in the code.
 SCHATZLINE: So is the section to instruct doctors to give the baby an
 opportunity to survive if the mother is facing a life-threatening condition? Is that
 the purpose of that section?
 GEREN: The purpose of that section, if the baby is born during the procedure or
 the baby is born before the procedure can be performed, is for the doctors to use
 their medical judgment to help the baby survive. That is correct.
 SCHATZLINE: Okay. So does this section instruct doctors to try, let ’s say, an
 early induction or C-section if possible, even if the mother is facing a
 life-threatening condition? Is that the idea?
 GEREN: It is, but it leaves it to the reasonable medical judgment of the doctor,
 which is defined in code currently.
 SCHATZLINE: So would this section allow the doctors to perform abortions in
 non-life-threatening conditions?
 GEREN: If they are possibly life-threatening or they affect the major bodily
 functions, the doctor has the––if it ’s reasonable medical judgment, then they can
 perform an abortion.
 SCHATZLINE: When you say major bodily functions, could you help us identify
 maybe what that was? Because I think some of the concern is we want to support
 the bill, but some of the concern from us is, is there a major bodily function––let
 me just ask it like this. If a mother has a high chance of survival but future
 infertility is a risk, does this bill allow for the doctor to use their judgment to
 conduct an abortion?
 GEREN: Fertility, by the Supreme Court, has already been defined as a major
 bodily function. So, yes, they could, using reasonable medical judgment.
 SCHATZLINE: Okay. That ’s clarifying. Does life-threatening have to be
 imminent?
 GEREN: It does not, as I said in my opening remarks.
 SCHATZLINE: Okay. Final question from me. What ’s an example of something
 that is life-threatening but not imminent? Just so we can gain a better
 understanding.
 GEREN: Cancer treatment. So a doctor will be able––there were several
 examples over the last six or eight months putting this bill together where doctors
 would not perform cancer treatment on a pregnant woman. This will allow them
 to do cancer treatment if it ’s necessary.
 REPRESENTATIVE HARRIS DAVILA: I just wanted to get some more
 clarification when we ’re talking about life-threatening and the definition in this
 bill. My understanding is that it ’s a two-part test that you first have to prove that
 there is a life-threatening condition. And as long as that ’s proven, you go on to
 the rest of the bill. Is that correct?
5496             89th LEGISLATURE — REGULAR SESSION

 GEREN: That is correct.
 HARRIS DAVILA: Okay. So life-threatening has to be proven first. Then if that
 is not proven, nothing else in the bill comes into effect?
 GEREN: Life-threatening or impairing the major bodily functions of the mother,
 but that ’s right. That has to be done before any procedure takes place.
 HARRIS DAVILA: Right. So that ’s the first test. So as long as the situation is a
 life-threatening situation, then the rest of the bill kicks in, but not before. Right?
 GEREN: That is correct.
 REPRESENTATIVE MONEY: I ’m really just trying to understand this bill, and I
 want to support it. You ’ve got a lot of support behind it, but I just want to clarify.
 GEREN: Mr. Money, over the last six months or so, I ’ve needed a lot of
 clarification myself.
 MONEY: Well, thank you. We ’ve spent time talking with some of the same
 stakeholders you ’re talking with. Where I ’m coming from is I think that
 Texas i’current law is clear that the life of the mother should be protected. I think
 it is clear. I think the Supreme Court has supported that. I think the problem we ’re
 trying to solve is there have been some malicious interpretations of the existing
 law––which I think is a good law––that have left some doctors in a situation that
 they are unclear about what they are supposed to do. I think people that want to
 promote abortion have tried to make it murky what our current law is. So my
 question is to you: Is this law written to make sure that malicious actors won t’ be
 able to find loopholes to allow abortions that would not be allowed under our
 current law?
 GEREN: Medical malpractice is still allowed under this law, and the answer to
 that is yes. What we are trying to achieve here is a doctor under current law can
 go to jail for the rest of his life. A doctor under this current law can lose his
 medical license, and that ’s why some have been very indecisive. Lawyers that
 represent hospitals, or any other clinic where an emergency surgery may take
 place, are concerned and are afraid to advise a physician or a hospital that it is
 okay to proceed with that abortion. This law clarifies that and gives them some
 clarity so they can go forward without fear of going to jail for the rest of their
 lives.
 MONEY: Yes. And to be clear, I don t’ want anybody going to jail for doing
 whatever needs to be done to try to save the life of a mother who is pregnant.
 What I also don t’ want to do is shift the pendulum in the other direction so
 doctors are able to concoct or find a way to perform an abortion that they
 wouldn t’ be able to under our law. You r’ e saying that that has been addressed and
 taken care of?
 GEREN: That has been addressed. And as I said, medical malpractice law is not
 being changed by this. We are in no way promoting abortion on this. What we are
 trying to do is save the life or the major bodily functions of a mother. I get a little
Wednesday, May 21, 2025          HOUSE JOURNAL — 69th Day                             5497

 emotional about this. I ’ve got friends who watched their wives nearly bleed out
 and become septic. That s’ the driver in this bill. In fact, a staffer, a good friend of
 mine, that nearly happened to his wife just this year.
 MONEY: Right. And we ’ve seen those things in the news, and it ’s happened to
 people that we know. I certainly want there to be clarity on being able to protect
 mothers who are at risk. But what I don t’ want to say is they ’re emotionally at
 risk or that they could possibly, in some very remote chance, have some negative
 impact, so we ’re going to go ahead and kill a perfectly viable life.
 GEREN: In the bill, they have to use reasonable medical judgment, which has
 been defined and is supported by the medical board. If that ’s happening, a
 complaint can still be filed against a doctor for doing that. They would then have
 to show they used reasonable medical judgment in order to do that. This not only
 protects the doctor, but it protects the nurses, the technicians, whoever is involved
 in the procedure. I ’ve got a real good pro-life voting record in this house, and I
 want to maintain that. But I ’m going to fight to the end to make sure that we
 protect the doctors who are taking care of these mothers who might otherwise
 die.
 MONEY: Yes, and I want to make sure we do exactly what you just said we
 would do but that we don t’ leave an opening for doctors whose only goal is to try
 to help people have abortions. You understand that?
 GEREN: I do understand that, but I don t’ believe that this bill does that. I don t’
 believe that Senator Hughes would have been able to get it out of the senate 31-0
 if that were the case. I can assure you, knowing Senator Hughes for as long as I
 have, I promise you they ’ve addressed that.
 MONEY: I agree with you.
 GEREN: And I ’m not a lawyer.
 REPRESENTATIVE LOWE: Sir, I have a question about reasonable medical
 judgment. As you know, the many doctors that worked at Planned Parenthood
 killed tens of thousands, probably millions, of babies. They were doctors. So can
 you explain to me what reasonable medical judgment is?
 GEREN: If you don t’ mind, let me flip through my pages to get to that.
 Reasonable medical judgment has been ruled on by the Texas Supreme Court, so
 I ’m not sure that I have the definition in here, but I think I do. The definition is
 not currently in the bill, but the Supreme Court has previously ruled on
 reasonable medical judgment. I ’d be happy to get that for you, but I do not have it
 in front of me now.
 LOWE: Okay. There ’s another section of the bill that says life-threatening means
 capable of causing death or potentially fatal––
 GEREN: Or substantial impairment of the major bodily functions?
 LOWE: Yes. "A life-threatening physical condition that is not necessarily one
 actively injuring the patient."
 GEREN: Cancer treatment is a perfect example of that.
5498             89th LEGISLATURE — REGULAR SESSION

 LOWE: Yes, and I get that. But as you have seen, science has really advanced,
 and we have been able to accomplish amazing things. Babies at 26 weeks have
 been able to live. Do you believe that we should allow a doctor to terminate––kill
 a human being––over cancer?
 GEREN: Can I go back to your first question?
 LOWE: Yes, sir. Absolutely.
 GEREN: Reasonable medical judgment is what a board-certified obstetrician
 believes will save the life of a mother.
 LOWE: Do you know how many, if any, doctors have lost their licenses or gone
 to prison because they ’ve performed an abortion?
 GEREN: I do not, sir. The law was passed––the Heartbeat Act is not that old, so I
 don t’ know how many have been prosecuted.
 LOWE: Okay. This bill creates loopholes to recharge the pro-choice industry in
 Texas, and I will be voting no.
 REPRESENTATIVE PIERSON: You had mentioned a bunch of pro-life
 organizations that support this bill, and the Human Coalition would be one of
 those organizations. Are you aware that I sat on the board of the Human Coalition
 before joining this chamber?
 GEREN: I was not aware of that. No, ma ’am.
 PIERSON: Yes. I proudly joined that organization because they do wonderful
 things in the communities to serve––not just saving babies but also the women
 who choose to keep their children. Are you aware that during the senate
 testimony, the Human Coalition testified on this bill and testified that due to the
 Texas Supreme Court clarifications, even though they registered for the bill, that
 this bill was unnecessary? Are you aware of that?
 GEREN: No, I am not aware of that. But I know that they ’ve signed off on this
 bill.
 PIERSON: Okay. Thank you. I just wanted to clarify for the record.
 HARRIS DAVILA: I just wanted to go back and clarify a little bit more, again,
 going back to the life-threatening side of this and going back to what the bill says
 in this section that defines life-threatening. So can you just go through––it ’s very
 specific to the pregnancy, correct?
 GEREN: That is correct.
 HARRIS DAVILA: And from what I ’m seeing, it says, "a life-threatening
 physical condition aggravated by, caused by, or arising from a pregnancy that
 places the female at risk of death or poses a serious risk of substantial impairment
 of a major bodily function." And so, again, we still have to prove that it ’s
 life-threatening, correct?
 GEREN: Yes, but it is based on the doctor ’s good judgment. It is not based on
 somebody here saying, "Well, she might die."
Wednesday, May 21, 2025         HOUSE JOURNAL — 69th Day                            5499

 HARRIS DAVILA: Right. And can you clarify a little bit more about the major
 bodily function? What kind of situations are we talking about? I know you had
 mentioned cancer before. What other situations will we see this?
 GEREN: Kidney failure, liver failure, becoming septic, which is a terrible
 infection. My father suffered from sepsis, and that ’s when he died. But
 we––today, doctors have waited actually until a woman has become septic, and
 that can cause her kidneys to fail and cause her liver to fail. So the doctor has to
 decide if this woman is going to die or she ’s not going to be able to live without
 being on dialysis for the rest of her life or need a transplant.
 HARRIS DAVILA: Exactly. And would you agree that sometimes there may not
 always be––going back to the imminent threat, you may not be bleeding out at
 the time, but there are indications of sepsis and other issues like that beforehand,
 right?
 GEREN: Yes, that ’s correct.
 HARRIS DAVILA: Right. And that ’s why we have some of that language in the
 bill. Thank you. I appreciate the clarification.
 REPRESENTATIVE SLAWSON: You and I worked together with our colleagues
 in the 87th Session, did we not, to protect innocent life?
 GEREN: That ’s correct. When you carried the bill.
 SLAWSON: And we intended at that time to also protect mothers, didn t’ we?
 GEREN: Yes, we did. In fact, that was a major point in your opening comments if
 I recall.
 SLAWSON: Yes. And your bill is designed to clarify that today, correct?
 GEREN: It is because of the medical board and the Supreme Court. Excuse me.
 The Supreme Court, as well as TMA, asked for clarifying rules from the medical
 board. We didn t’ get them. Or we got them, and nobody really knew what they
 said. So that ’s why we ’re doing this bill because of the failure of the medical
 board to come up with reasonable, understandable rules. And I believe that we
 have that here.

 for this, having worked with him before. I appreciate you taking up that mantle in

 GEREN: This will not provide an excuse for an abortion.
 CAIN: Yes, but will more or less babies die? Is it going to save lives or not?

 REPRESENTATIVE OLIVERSON: Chairman, I appreciate you bringing this
 bill. You probably aren t’ aware of this, but I get this question all the time as a

5502             89th LEGISLATURE — REGULAR SESSION

 truly heartbreaking scenarios, and I ’m sure you ’ve probably heard them too. Are
 you aware that in a lot of circumstances, a mother who is pregnant with an infant
 who is pre-viable––in other words, if that baby was delivered at that moment in
 time, there ’s 100 percent certainty that baby will not survive––begins to exhibit
 signs and symptoms of a medical condition that will be life-threatening? Have
 you heard these stories?
 GEREN: Yes, I have.
 OLIVERSON: Okay. Are you aware that when somebody is in that particular
 circumstance, that there are doctors that know what the right thing to do is, which

 the pregnancy. Have you heard those stories?
 GEREN: I have. And if the mother dies, the baby is going to die.
 OLIVERSON: One hundred percent of the time, and especially in these
 circumstances. So the issue that I think that this bill is trying to address is these
 situations where a pregnancy has not yet reached the level of fetal development
 where the baby can exist outside of the womb, but because of complications of
 pregnancy, the mother is no longer able to sustain the pregnancy without losing
 her own life. Is that the purpose of what you ’re trying to do here?
 GEREN: That ’s the purpose. Tom, honestly, some of the stories I ’ve heard over

 think how it affected the mother and the family. These women are not going there
 to get rid of a baby. They ’re going there to save their lives. And the doctors are
 performing the abortion in order to save the woman s’ life. Once again, if the
 mother dies, the baby is going to die.
 OLIVERSON: Are you aware that a lot of these medical conditions, like
 hemorrhage and infection, are things where physicians are looking at the law as
 written, and the issue boils down to the fact that in their medical judgment there
 is a 100 percent likelihood that ultimately that woman is going to die from the
 signs and symptoms and the diagnosis of whatever condition is beginning to
 manifest, but they r’ e not dying yet?
 GEREN: I would agree with that.
 OLIVERSON: So what you ’re trying to allow for is, in these circumstances, for a

 GEREN: Yes, sir. And you ’ve taken the Hippocratic Oath.
 OLIVERSON: Yes, sir.
 GEREN: A doctor has taken that and is looking there and saying, "I don t’ know
 what to do. I may go to jail for the rest of my life if I perform this, but if I wait a

 period of time.

 emergency––are not reasons for an abortion unless it ’s going to be

 us.

 know I ’m not normally on the microphone this long.

                             REASONS FOR VOTE
       Representative Cain submitted the following reason for vote to be printed in
 the journal:
      I have dedicated much of my professional and political life to fighting to end
 abortion. There is a lot of confusion around this bill as to whether it will save
 more babies from being murdered. At the time of voting, the clerk ’s office
 showed that there were 37 democrat sponsors or co-sponsors compared to just
 22irepublican sponsors or co-sponsors. This alone made me skeptical of the
5508             89th LEGISLATURE — REGULAR SESSION

 impact of this bill. Because I was unable to determine whether or not
 SBi31iwould lead to an increase or reduction in the killing of the unborn, I voted
 present, not voting.
       Representative Holt submitted the following reason for vote to be printed in
 the journal:
      I voted present, not voting on SBi31 because I have concerns about the
 unintended implications of SBi31. I intend to seek feedback from my constituents
 and make a firm vote on third reading.
      Representative Leo Wilson submitted the following reason for vote to be
 printed in the journal:
      As a committed pro-life legislator, I have always sought to uphold and
 defend the sanctity of life at every stage. I originally signed on to SBi31 in good
 faith, based on the strong support it received from respected pro-life
 organizations, including Texas Right to Life, Texas Alliance for Life, and the
 Human Coalition. However, after today ’s layout of the bill, I have serious
 concerns about its current form and whether this legislation is still necessary.
 While I will remain signed on to the bill at this time out of respect for our life
 coalition partners, I am reserving judgment and may reconsider my position
 depending on how the bill is presented on third reading.
      Representatives Vasut, Alders, and Swanson submitted the following reason
 for vote to be printed in the journal:
       I believe every human being is made in the image of God and has inherent
 value and immeasurable worth from the moment of fertilization. Every human
 life matters: born and unborn, baby and mom. I originally signed on to
 SBi31ibecause it was signed off on by Texas Right to Life and Human Coalition.
 Based upon the floor debate and floor debate alone, I am currently unsure if the
 bill is necessary and have questions about some of the bill language. I have
 decided to vote present, not voting at this time because if I vote yes, that may be
 misconstrued as a vote to allow for more babies to die, which I oppose. If I vote
 no, that may be misconstrued as a vote not to protect the life of the mother, which
 I, of course, want to protect.

                        SB 33 ON SECOND READING
                   (Noble, Leo Wilson, et al. - House Sponsors)
      SB 33, A bill to be entitled An Act relating to certain prohibited transactions
 and logistical support between a governmental entity and an abortion assistance
 entity or abortion provider for the procurement of an abortion or related services.
      (Alders, Gates, Hunter, Morgan, and Virdell now present)
                                SB 33 - REMARKS
 REPRESENTATIVE NOBLE: SBi33 is a taxpayer protection bill. Members,
 three sessions ago, during the 86th Legislature, we passed and signed into law the
 prohibition of local and state tax dollars from being used to fund abortion
 providers and their affiliates. The intent of the bill was very clear, as evidenced
 by the seven and a half hours of debate we had on the house floor. Taxpayers
 should not have their tax dollars fund an activity that is illegal in Texas.
 Unfortunately, at least one city in Texas found what they considered to be a
 loophole in our law, and they ’ve allocated taxpayer dollars to nonprofits
 specifically to pay for Texas women to travel out of state for the purpose of an
 abortion. Tax dollars should not facilitate travel to another state for an act that is
 not allowed here. SBi33 is a cleanup bill to close a perceived loophole and to
 make our legislative intent very clear.
 REPRESENTATIVE HOWARD: The prohibition in this bill applies even if cities
 are only providing support to organizations that assist Texans to travel out of
 state. Is that correct?
 NOBLE: The way that the cities passed their grant—they consider it to be
 grants—the way I understand it was that it was specifically for the purpose of
 having women go out of state in order to obtain abortions.
 HOWARD: They ’re providing support to the organizations. Is that correct?
 NOBLE: That is the purpose of the grant.
 HOWARD: It applies if cities are providing support to an organization that helps
 rape victims leave the state to get an abortion in a state where it ’s legal, is that
 correct?
 NOBLE: Going to do something that ’s not allowed in Texas—to use taxpayer
 dollars to go do it in another state—
5510             89th LEGISLATURE — REGULAR SESSION

 HOWARD: Where it is legal?
 NOBLE: Again, you ’re using Texas tax dollars to do something that is not
 allowed in our state. You ’re spending Texas tax dollars to do that in another state.
 That is unacceptable.
 HOWARD: So helping rape victims would not be okay? It applies if cities are
 providing support to an organization that helps women with fatal fetal diagnoses
 leave the state to get an abortion in a state where it s’ legal.
 NOBLE: I think we cleared up a lot of those questions in the bill we just passed.
 HOWARD: But for your bill?
 NOBLE: My bill does not conflict with the bill that we just passed. In fact, it
 does really well with it, I think.
 HOWARD: In other words, cities could not provide support to an organization
 that helps women with fatal fetal diagnoses leave the state to get an abortion in a
 state where it ’s legal?
 NOBLE: I do not believe that we should be doing that. No.
 HOWARD: Does it apply if cities are providing support to an organization that
 helps incest survivors leave the state?
 NOBLE: I don t’ know if this is part of this bill, but I will tell you that one of my
 best friends in college was the result of incest. His life is such a valuable and
 wonderful part of my life and has been for many decades. His life is no less
 valuable because of his start.
                          ’ not implying that at all.
 HOWARD: Oh absolutely, I m
 NOBLE: Well, you sound like he was a mistake that should have been taken care
 of, and I absolutely disagree because of that.
 HOWARD: I ’m sorry. I will clarify to you because that is not my intent. What my
 belief is is that it ’s up to a person and their individual circumstances to make that
 decision.
 NOBLE: Again, it is not the job of our state tax dollars to make that—provide for
 that.
 HOWARD: Does the prohibition in your bill apply to logistical support? Not just
 to money? Does it apply to childcare, travel, transportation, lodging, food,
 counseling, and any other service?
 NOBLE: It does. In fact, it does everything that ’s included in that tourism level of
 providing for someone an abortion in another state.
 HOWARD: What does "any other service mean"—besides those I just listed?
 NOBLE: Those are the ones that are specifically listed in the bill.
 HOWARD: What ’s "any other service"? That ’s in the bill too.
 NOBLE: Well, I guess that we ’re just trying to make sure that we cover
 any—another perceived loophole that someone might come up with.
Wednesday, May 21, 2025        HOUSE JOURNAL — 69th Day                          5511

 HOWARD: Does the prohibition apply to food pantries?
 NOBLE: You know, you may not know this. I actually ran a food pantry for
 17iyears as a volunteer. That is something that is very near and dear to my heart.
 Providing for someone—for their needs for today and tomorrow—has nothing to
 do with this bill.
 HOWARD: But I think we just said that it prohibits logistical support, such as
 food.
 NOBLE: Well, that is food specifically for the travel out of state for the purpose
 of an abortion.
 HOWARD: And if a food pantry did not know they were providing food to a
 person that was traveling out of state to get an abortion, would it apply—
 NOBLE: I think that ’s really grasping for some interesting legal arguments, but I
 never asked someone when they came to my food pantry what they were needing
 that for.
 HOWARD: Yet in this bill, is there not a private cause of action that anybody
 could determine that it was the case and could still sue the food pantry?
 NOBLE: The cause of action is against a city that is specifically doing these
 things for the purpose of an abortion.
 HOWARD: The city might be supporting the food pantry.
 NOBLE: Again, a food pantry is a different animal than logistical support for an
 abortion.
 HOWARD: Does this apply to pastoral counseling?
 NOBLE: I do not understand where you ’re going with that.
 HOWARD: If support is being provided to religious organizations from a city,
 and the counseling—
 NOBLE: For the purpose of going out of state? I don t’ understand how a pastoral
 counselor would tell someone to go out of state for an abortion.
 HOWARD: There are multiple viewpoints among clergy as much as there are
 among the body we have here. There are clergy who would support their—
 NOBLE: Again, I don t’ believe that clergy talking to someone within their body
 of believers would be part of logistical support for someone traveling out of the
 state for an abortion.
 HOWARD: That ’s what we ’re trying to clear up here, so thank you for that.
 NOBLE: Again, that grant money was set up very differently than the way you ’re
 describing this for the cities that we ’re talking about.
 HOWARD: So you wouldn t’ be able to assert a defense for religious freedom,
 right? If you were in a religious organization that receives support from the city,
 and you offered counseling and assistance to one of the people that attend your
 religious organization—
5512             89th LEGISLATURE — REGULAR SESSION

 NOBLE: Are you talking about getting in the car and driving them to another
 state and using gas money? I ’m a little confused about where clergy might be
 involved in that.
 HOWARD: I think clergy could be involved in all kinds of things—in all kinds of
 assistance.
 NOBLE: Well, if they are indeed getting in a car with them, driving them to
 another state, and using taxpayer funds—again, back to the taxpayer funds for
 that purpose—then that would be included.
 HOWARD: Which entities would this bill actually affect? Does it refer to
 affiliates?
 NOBLE: Any entity that would be—in fact, it ’s really interesting that you would
 ask because the two cities that have done this have had a number of entities ask
 them for that grant money. So I mean, that s’ not an exhaustive list.
 HOWARD: Okay, but does it apply to affiliates of abortion providers?
 NOBLE: The former bill did and probably would on this as well. But again, this
 is specifically the logistical support to travel out of state for the purpose of an
 abortion.
 HOWARD: Does it have anything to do with beyond procuring an abortion? Is it
 strictly for procuring an abortion?
 NOBLE: I ’m not advised because I ’m not sure what you mean by that question.

 NOBLE: We ’ve spent a great deal of time as a legislature trying to figure out

 local taxpayers.
 REPRESENTATIVE WU: In Section 4, page 4iof your bill, starting in Subsection

 action—a defendant may not use Rule 91aiof the Texas Rules of Civil Procedure.
 Are you aware of what that rule does?
 NOBLE: I ’m not advised.
 WU: You do know that the rule in the Texas Rules of Civil Procedure is called
 the "dismissal of baseless causes of action," correct?
 NOBLE: I ’m not advised.
 WU: Okay. Sorry, you ’re not sure, or you don t’ know?
 NOBLE: I ’m not looking at that part of the code.
 WU: Rule 91aisays that the court may not award attorney costs or attorney fees
 under Rulei91a. Rule 91aisays that if somebody files a frivolous lawsuit—a
 baseless lawsuit—that means that there is no rhyme or reason that they should be
 filing it, and the AG, or the person that is allowed to do this, can just—
 NOBLE: You do realize that the amendment we just adopted narrowed it down to
 just the AG? You do understand that, correct?
 WU: Correct. You understand that the AG in the statute—in the existing statute,
 the AG has the ability to recover costs. Correct?
 NOBLE: I am not advised in that statute. I ’m not reading that statute as you are.
5514            89th LEGISLATURE — REGULAR SESSION

 WU: No, not Texas Rule of Civil Procedure, literally the statute that you are
 amending right now—Section 2273.004iof the Government Code.
 NOBLE: Yes. Our goal, of course, is not to have any action. Our goal is to have
 cities not doing this, and therefore there is no need for a cause of action.
 WU: Yes, of course. In the existing law—the existing law of that statute that we
 have already put in place many sessions ago—the existing law provides the
 attorney general with the ability to recover court costs. You ’re aware of that?
 NOBLE: Correct.
 WU: But on the flip side, you are now purposefully denying the city the ability to
 recover court costs for a frivolous suit—even for a frivolous suit.
 NOBLE: I would be very sad if we thought that our attorney general was
 bringing a frivolous suit. I certainly—that is certainly not the will of this
 legislature, and I will again say this: The goal is to keep cities from using
 taxpayer funds to transport women out of state for something that is not allowed
 in Texas.
 WU: I understand that we wish and hope for many things, but part of the point of
 rules is that we establish guidelines that say if something happens, there is a
 remedy for it—there is a protection for it. But you ’re specifically taking that
 protection out, correct?
 NOBLE: I am keeping my bill as it came from the senator, yes.
 WU: Okay. And in Subsection (c-2), you specifically strike out Chapters 27iand
 110iof the Civil Practices and Remedies Code. Are you aware of what those
 chapters do?
 NOBLE: I do not have those in front of me.

 the Texas anti-SLAPP statute?
 NOBLE: Again, I do not have that part of the code in front of me.
 WU: Okay, well let me help you out just a tiny bit. In Chapter 27—the stated
 purpose of Chapter 27iis—it says in statutei27.002—"the purpose of this chapter
 is to encourage and safeguard the constitutional rights of persons to petition,
 speak freely, associate freely, and otherwise participate in government to the
 maximum extent permitted by law and, at the same time, protect the rights of a
 person to file meritorious lawsuits for demonstrable injury." This law, if passed,
 would prohibit the cities from this purpose.
 NOBLE: Again, my goal is to have cities comply with the law. Absolutely. They
 must comply with this law. That is our goal, that is the goal of our legislature
 every time we pass a law, and yes, that is the goal of this law.
 WU: But you ’re not going to—you ’re not arguing with me that it doesn t’ restrict
 cities from the application of this chapter, right?
 NOBLE: I am going to stay within the four corners of this bill and stick with the
 wording that was sent to me by Senator Campbell.
Wednesday, May 21, 2025        HOUSE JOURNAL — 69th Day                            5515

 WU: The wording of this bill is that cities, if they ’re sued, may not use
 protections of personal freedom and public freedom as a defense.
 NOBLE: Our goal, once again, is to never have a city be sued because they are so
 in compliance with this law and other laws of this state.
 WU: Are you familiar with Chapter 110iof the Texas Civil Practices and
 Remedies Code?
 NOBLE: Again, I do not have that in front of me.
 WU: Do you know that Chapter 110iis called the Religious Freedom Protection
 Act?
 NOBLE: I will take your word for it.

 NOBLE: I cannot fathom a time and place where a city would need to use a

 abortion. I cannot fathom that in Texas.
 WU: Well, that ’s why we have robust debates on the floor because I might bring

 REPRESENTATIVE GÁMEZ: Representative Noble, understanding that your
 bill expressly strips these entities against the defense—anti-SLAPP and religious
 protections—what defenses are available to organizations accused of violating
 this law?
 NOBLE: Again, we ’re not talking about organizations. We ’re talking specifically
 of cities that violate the law as we pass it. Our goal always in this room is to have
 our laws followed when we pass them, not for a city to just find another
 loophole—or another perceived loophole. That ’s why this one is so clear. That ’s
 why we felt like we needed to make the penalty so clear, because we don t’ want
 them just finding another perceived loophole.
 GÁMEZ: And I do agree with you—the penalties are clear. But are any defenses
 enumerated in your bill for cities?
 NOBLE: I am not advised. I have not looked at that entire code.
 GÁMEZ: There are none. What specifically is the burden of proof now of the
 attorney general to prove once he or she has lodged an accusation like this against
 a city?
 NOBLE: To my understanding, it ’s the preponderance of the evidence.

 understanding of the bill—
 NOBLE: You know, I am not an attorney, nor do I play one on TV. I am also not a

 this ever need to come to a courtroom, but I don t’ believe it will be because—
 GÁMEZ: I have no doubt that it will be litigated immediately.
 NOBLE: I believe that it s’ so clear that the cities will comply and that your
 arguments will be unneeded and unnecessary.
 GÁMEZ: Are you aware that your bill does not enumerate any specific mens rea
 or intent on behalf of a city to allegedly be in violation of this law?
 NOBLE: That would be between them, the court, and the attorney general.
 GÁMEZ: So again, what is your—in your opinion, based on the way you ’ve
 written this—or you ’ve agreed to carry the language in this bill—what
 specifically does the attorney general have to prove to show that a city has
 violated this bill? Would you like someone else to answer for you or—
 NOBLE: No. I ’m not an attorney, so I ’m just making sure I ’m not messing up on

 GÁMEZ: You ’d agree with me that your bill does not enumerate any intent or

 GÁMEZ: So your goal is specifically—

 GÁMEZ: All right. I thought you weren t’ an attorney. Did you just decide to

 NOBLE: I ’m not; somebody just told me that. Aren t’ they cool? They ’re so

 GÁMEZ: An attorney did tell you that, okay. Again, understanding that—so we

 NOBLE: As written.

 there any reason you ’ve made an exception for that in this case?
Wednesday, May 21, 2025          HOUSE JOURNAL — 69th Day                             5517

 clear intent of a law that we passed in the 86th Legislature, it ’s time to get a little
 more stern. That ’s what we have done.

 NOBLE: Protecting taxpayers ’rights.

                         SB 269 ON SECOND READING
                             (Frank - House Sponsor)
      SB 269, A bill to be entitled An Act relating to required reports of certain
 vaccine-related or drug-related adverse events.
              SB 269 - STATEMENT OF LEGISLATIVE INTENT
 REPRESENTATIVE FRANK: Despite having the word "vaccine" in the caption,
 this is a pretty noncontroversial bill that came out of the senate 31-0 and Public
 Healthi11-1. What it does is it ’s a medical reporting bill that helps ensure that
 Texas doctors are reporting serious adverse reactions related to experimental,
 investigational, or emergency-use vaccines or drugs. Either one—there s’ actually
 both. What the bill does is—it really is a reporting bill. It requires physicians to
 report any serious adverse reactions. Those are in the bill. Where they report
 them—there ’s two federal ways to report them. One is the VERS—Vaccine Event
 Reporting System. The second is MedWatch for the drugs. Again, those are
 already set up in federal statute. This is just trying to make sure that we have that
 information. Everybody wants to make sure this is safe. When do they have to do
 it? Really, they only have to do it if they diagnose a patient with it. If they know a
 patient has received a vaccination and the patient suffers serious adverse
 reactions. Those are literally laid out in the bill, but they are very serious
 reactions. I could walk through those.
       I do have a perfecting amendment that I ’d like to get on there that TMA
 asked me to do. It ’s a very simple amendment. You can ask Representative
 Zwiener. It literally flips two words. It ’s something TMA, who looked at the bill,
 would like. If I could put that on and then answer questions, that would be great.
 REPRESENTATIVE WU: Can I just ask you some legislative intent questions?
 FRANK: Absolutely.
 WU: Could you help walk me through the process of this? I understand that your
 bill, right now, is very limited in scope.
 FRANK: Yes.
 WU: It is only talking about things resulting in death, life-threatening conditions,
 and things that have resulted in permanent disabilities––
 FRANK: Inpatient hospitalization––
 WU: Severe stuff.
 FRANK: Yes, severe stuff.
 WU: Not, like, they got a rash.
5522             89th LEGISLATURE — REGULAR SESSION

 FRANK: Not a tremendous headache. Really the most open one, if you will, is
 "results in a medically important condition that, based on the physician ’s
 reasonable medical judgment, may require medical or surgical intervention to
 prevent" one of those more serious things—death or what have you.
 WU: Is it your clear intent to have a very limited scope for when this applies?
 FRANK: Yes, to follow the bill. Yes.
 WU: The bill itself is very limited?
 FRANK: The bill is limited to what it ’s limited by. It ’s limited to––
 WU: Severe cases?
 FRANK: Yes, severe cases.
 WU: I think we ’re just trying to get to the same point.
 FRANK: It is both drugs and vaccines, though. To that degree, they are two
 different things. It is experimental drugs and approved vaccines.
 WU: Yes, in these limited situations. And the drug or vaccine has to be
 experimental, correct?
 FRANK: Correct.
 WU: It has to be something that the FDA has designated as an emergency use or
 an experimental use.
 FRANK: Correct.
 WU: And that is black-letter in your bill?
 FRANK: Correct.
 WU: Okay. If a physician encounters a situation where someone was given an
 experimental drug and, let ’s say, God forbid, they pass away—does the physician
 have to know, or state that they believe, that the patient died from the application
 of the experimental medicine, or merely that the person took the experimental
 medicine and then subsequently, at some point, passed away?
 FRANK: The latter. There ’s no way for them to know cause and effect.
 WU: Correct.
 FRANK: Again, these two federal registries, if you will––I may not be calling
 them the right thing––the two places you ’re supposed to do this are voluntary.
 What we are doing in statute is saying this is a responsibility of the doctors. The
 state is not going to be overseeing this; the medical board is. There ’s no cause of
 action or anything. The medical board is going to be the one responsible for that.
 WU: The point I want to get to is: You ’re not asking doctors to make judgment
 calls about whether the drug and/or vaccine is responsible for the injuries or the
 death, but merely that it coincided?
 FRANK: Right. Yes. We are wanting data.
Wednesday, May 21, 2025        HOUSE JOURNAL — 69th Day                          5523

                       REMARKS ORDERED PRINTED
      Representative Wu moved to print all remarks on SBi269.
      The motion prevailed.
 Amendment No. 1
      Representative Frank offered the following amendment to SBi269:
      Amend SBi269 (house committee report) by striking "approved or
 authorized" and substituting "authorized or approved" in each of the following
 places the phrase appears:
      (1)iipage 2, line 1; and
      (2)iipage 3, line 23.
                      AMENDMENT NO. 1 - REMARKS
 FRANK: This truly is a perfecting amendment. It was requested by TMA. It
 literally switches two words. It is, obviously, acceptable to the author.
      Amendment No. 1 was adopted.
 Amendment No. 2
      Representative Tinderholt offered the following amendment to SBi269:
      Amend SBi269 (house committee report) by striking page 1, line 24,
 through page 2, line 2, and substituting the following:
          (1)iiexperimental or investigational;
          (2)iiapproved or authorized for emergency use by the United States
 Food and Drug Administration; or
          (3)iirecommended for use by the Advisory Committee on Immunization
 Practices of the federal Centers for Disease Control and Prevention.
                      AMENDMENT NO. 2 - REMARKS
 REPRESENTATIVE TINDERHOLT: This is pretty simple. It adds all vaccines to
 it. The reason that I want to do that is because when my son got the MMR
 vaccine, he was nonverbal for about 10idays after and then got better. I really
 want both sides to consider this because it creates data. It allows us to have data
 on all vaccines and make informed decisions. One side says there are no vaccine
 injuries. The other side says there is. I just want the data collected so we can
 make quality decisions for our children and people across the state.
 Amendment No. 2 - Point of Order
       Representative Wu raised a point of order against further consideration of
 Amendment No. 2 under Rule 11, Section 2, of the House Rules on the grounds
 that the amendment is not germane. The point of order was withdrawn.
      Amendment No. 2 was withdrawn.
                               SB 269 - REMARKS
 FRANK: If we want to follow the science, we actually need to have the data. This
 allows us to get more data about these.

Wednesday, May 21, 2025         HOUSE JOURNAL — 69th Day                             5529

 Amendment No. 1
      Representative Spiller offered the following amendment to SBi740:
       Amend SBi740 (house committee report) by striking page 6, line 25,
 through page 7, line 8, and substituting the following:
       (g)iiNotwithstanding Section 64.021, Civil Practice and Remedies Code, a
 receiver appointed under this section may be a person, a municipally owned
 utility, a county, a water supply or sewer service corporation, a public utility
 agency, or a district or authority created under Section 52, Article III, or Section
 59, Article XVI, Texas Constitution, and may seek approval from the utility
 commission and the commission to acquire the water or sewer utility ’s facilities
 and transfer the utility ’s certificate of convenience and necessity. The receiver
 must apply in accordance with Subchapter H.
      Amendment No. 1 was adopted.
 Amendment No. 2
      Representative Tepper offered the following amendment to SBi740:
      Amend SBi740 (house committee report) by adding the following
 appropriately numbered SECTIONS to the bill and renumbering subsequent
 SECTIONS of the bill accordingly:
      SECTIONi____.iiSections 12.013(b) and (d), Water Code, are amended to
 read as follows:
      (b)iiIn this section, "political subdivision" means municipalities
 [incorporated cities, towns or villages], counties, river authorities, water districts,
 and other special purpose districts.
      (d)iiThe utility commission ’s jurisdiction under this section relating to a
 municipality [incorporated cities, towns, or villages] shall be limited to water
 furnished by the municipality [such city, town, or village] to another political
 subdivision, other than another municipality, on a wholesale basis.
      SECTIONi____.iiSection 13.043, Water Code, is amended by adding
 Subsection (f-1) and amending Subsection (j) to read as follows:
      (f-1)iiSubsection (f) does not apply to a decision of a municipality regarding
 wholesale water or sewer service provided to another municipality.
      (j)iiIn an appeal under this section, the utility commission shall ensure that
 every appealed rate is just and reasonable. Rates shall not be unreasonably
 preferential, prejudicial, or discriminatory but shall be sufficient, equitable, and
 consistent in application to each class of customers. The utility commission shall
 use a methodology that preserves the financial integrity of the retail public utility.
 [For agreements between municipalities the utility commission shall consider the
 terms of any wholesale water or sewer service agreement in an appellate rate
 proceeding.]
      Amendment No. 2 was adopted.
     SB 740, as amended, was passed to third reading by (Record 3173): 142
 Yeas, 0 Nays, 3 Present, not voting.

                         CSSB 3 ON SECOND READING
                             (King - House Sponsor)
      CSSB 3, A bill to be entitled An Act relating to the regulation of products
 derived from hemp, including consumable hemp products and hemp beverages
 and the hemp-derived cannabinoids contained in those products; requiring
 occupational licenses and permits; imposing fees; creating criminal offenses;
 authorizing civil penalties; imposing taxes.
      CSSB 3 was read second time on May 20, postponed until 3ip.m. May 20,
 postponed until 6ip.m. May 20, postponed until 4ip.m. today, and was again
 postponed until this time.

      Sec.i443.2026.iiCONSUMABLE HEMP PRODUCT REGISTRATION. (a)
 A consumable hemp product may not be offered for sale in this state unless the
 manufacturer of the product, before selling the product to a retailer:
           (1)iisubmits an application for the consumable hemp product to be
 registered with the department; and
           (2)iireceives approval that the product is compliant with this chapter,

Wednesday, May 21, 2025        HOUSE JOURNAL — 69th Day                            5557

       (b)iiThe department shall issue a unique product registration number to each
 consumable hemp product approved by the department.
       (c)iiA manufacturer applying to register a consumable hemp product under
 this section shall pay an application fee to the department in the amount of
 $500ifor each consumable hemp product.

 the department ’s public Internet website, which must include front and back
 identifying pictures of each registered product.

            (1)iia QR code that links:

 product by law enforcement; and
            (2)iithe following message placed adjacent to the required QR code on
 the label: "SCAN QR CODE BEFORE PURCHASE".
       (f)iiThe department may not approve for sale a consumable hemp product
 that:
            (1)iicontains any artificial or synthetic cannabinoids; or
            (2)iicontains or is mixed with any alcohol, tobacco, nicotine, kratom,
 kava, mushrooms, or a derivative of any of those items.
       (g)iiA person commits an offense if the person distributes, delivers, sells,
 purchases, possesses, or uses a consumable hemp product that is not registered
 with the department as provided by this section.
       (h)iiAn offense under this section is a Class B misdemeanor.
       (i)iiA person is presumed to know a consumable hemp product is prohibited
 under this chapter if the product is not listed on the department ’s Internet website
 as required by Subsection (d) or does not have a valid QR code under Subsection
 (e).
       (j)iiThe executive commissioner shall adopt rules to implement and
 administer this section.

 amended by adding Sections 443.2055 and 443.2056 to read as follows:
      Sec.i443.2055.iiOFFENSE: MARKETING OF CONSUMABLE HEMP
 PRODUCT OR PACKAGING IN MANNER ATTRACTIVE TO MINORS. (a)
 A person commits an offense if the person markets, advertises, sells, or causes to
 be sold an edible consumable hemp product containing a hemp-derived
 cannabinoid that:
           (1)iiis in the shape of a human, animal, fruit, or cartoon or in another
 shape that is attractive to children; or
           (2)iiis in packaging or a container that:
                (A)iiis in the shape of a human, animal, fruit, or cartoon or in
 another shape that is attractive to children;
                (B)iidepicts an image of a human, animal, fruit, or cartoon or
 another image that is attractive to children;
                (C)iiimitates or mimics trademarks or trade dress of products that
 are or have been primarily marketed to minors;
                (D)iiincludes a symbol that is primarily used to market products to
 minors;
                (E)iiincludes an image of a celebrity; or
                (F)iiincludes an image that resembles a food product, including
 candy or juice.
      (b)iiIn this section, a cartoon includes a depiction of an object, person,
 animal, creature, or any similar caricature that:
           (1)iiuses comically exaggerated features and attributes;
           (2)iiassigns human characteristics to animals, plants, or other objects; or
           (3)iihas unnatural or extra-human abilities, such as imperviousness to
 pain or injury, x-ray vision, tunneling at very high speeds, or transformation.
      (c)iiAn offense under this section is a Class A misdemeanor.
      Sec.i443.2056.iiOFFENSE: MISLEADING CONSUMABLE HEMP
 PACKAGING. (a) A person commits an offense if the person sells or offers for
 sale a consumable hemp product that contains or is marketed as containing
 hemp-derived cannabinoids in a package that depicts any statement, artwork, or
 design that would likely mislead a person to believe:
5560             89th LEGISLATURE — REGULAR SESSION

           (1)iithe package does not contain a hemp-derived cannabinoid; or
           (2)iithe product is intended for medical use, including by depicting a
 green cross.
      (b)iiAn offense under this section is a Class A misdemeanor.

      SECTIONi17.iiChapter 443, Health and Safety Code, is amended by adding
 Subchapters F and G to read as follows:
                     SUBCHAPTER F. CRIMINAL OFFENSES
       Sec.i443.251.iiOFFENSE: MANUFACTURE, DELIVERY, OR
 POSSESSION WITH INTENT TO DELIVER OF CERTAIN CONSUMABLE
 HEMP PRODUCTS. (a) A person commits an offense if the person knowingly
 manufactures, delivers, or possesses with intent to deliver a consumable hemp
 product that contains any amount of a cannabinoid other than cannabidiol or
 cannabigerol.
      (b)iiAn offense under this section is a felony of the third degree.
      (c)iiIf conduct constituting an offense under this section also constitutes an
 offense under another law, the actor may be prosecuted under this section, the
 other law, or both.
      Sec.i443.252.iiOFFENSE: POSSESSION OF CERTAIN CONSUMABLE
 HEMP PRODUCTS. (a) A person commits an offense if the person knowingly or
 intentionally possesses a consumable hemp product that contains any amount of a
 cannabinoid other than cannabidiol or cannabigerol.
      (b)iiAn offense under this section is a Class A misdemeanor.
      (c)iiIf conduct constituting an offense under this section also constitutes an
 offense under another law, the actor may be prosecuted under this section, the
 other law, or both.
      Sec.i443.253.iiOFFENSE: SALE OR DISTRIBUTION OF CERTAIN
 CONSUMABLE HEMP PRODUCTS TO PERSONS YOUNGER THAN
 21iYEARS OF AGE; PROOF OF AGE REQUIRED. (a) A person commits an
Wednesday, May 21, 2025         HOUSE JOURNAL — 69th Day                             5561

 offense if the person, with criminal negligence, sells a consumable hemp product
 that contains or is marketed as containing hemp-derived cannabinoids to a person
 who is younger than 21 years of age.
       (b)iiAn employee of the owner of a store in which consumable hemp
 products that contain or are marketed as containing hemp-derived cannabinoids
 are sold at retail is criminally responsible and subject to prosecution for an
 offense under this section that occurs in connection with a sale by the employee.
       (c)iiAn offense under this section is a Class A misdemeanor.
       (d)iiIt is a defense to prosecution under Subsection (a) that the person to
 whom the consumable hemp product was sold presented to the defendant
 apparently valid proof of identification.
       (e)iiA proof of identification satisfies the requirements of Subsection (d) if it
 contains a physical description and photograph consistent with the person ’s
 appearance, purports to establish that the person is 21 years of age or older, and
 was issued by a governmental agency.iiThe proof of identification may include a
 driver ’s license issued by this state or another state, a passport, or an
 identification card issued by a state or the federal government.
       (f)iiThe owner of a store in which consumable hemp products that contain or
 are marketed as containing hemp-derived cannabinoids are sold, or an employee
 of the owner, may not display consumable hemp products for sale adjacent to
 products that are legal for children to consume.
       Sec.i443.254.iiOFFENSE: MANUFACTURE, DISTRIBUTION, OR SALE
 OF CONSUMABLE HEMP PRODUCTS FOR SMOKING. (a) A person
 commits an offense if the person manufactures, distributes, sells, or offers for sale
 a consumable hemp product for smoking.
       (b)iiAn offense under this section is a Class B misdemeanor.
       Sec.i443.255.iiOFFENSE: SALE OR DELIVERY OF CERTAIN
 CONSUMABLE HEMP PRODUCTS NEAR SCHOOL. (a) In this section,
 "school" and "premises" have the meanings assigned by Section 481.134.
       (b)iiA person commits an offense if the person sells, offers for sale, or
 delivers a consumable hemp product containing a hemp-derived cannabinoid in,
 on, or within 1,000 feet of the premises of a school.
       (c)iiAn offense under this section is a Class B misdemeanor.
       Sec.i443.256.iiOFFENSE: PROVISION OF CERTAIN CONSUMABLE
 HEMP PRODUCT BY COURIER, DELIVERY, OR MAIL SERVICE. (a) A
 person commits an offense if the person provides a consumable hemp product
 containing a hemp-derived cannabinoid by courier, delivery, or mail service.
       (b)iiAn offense under this section is a Class A misdemeanor.
       Sec.i443.257.iiOFFENSE: FALSE LABORATORY REPORT. (a) A person
 commits an offense if the person, with the intent to deceive, forges, falsifies, or
 alters the results of a laboratory test authorized or required by this chapter.
       (b)iiAn offense under this section is a felony of the third degree.
       Sec.i443.258.iiOFFENSE: MANUFACTURING OR SELLING WITHOUT
 LICENSE OR REGISTRATION. (a) A person commits an offense if the person:
            (1)iiprocesses hemp or manufactures a consumable hemp product
 without a license issued under Subchapter C; or
5562             89th LEGISLATURE — REGULAR SESSION

           (2)iisells at retail or offers for sale at retail a consumable hemp product
 without registering as a retailer under Section 443.2025.
      (b)iiAn offense under this section is a felony of the third degree.
             SUBCHAPTER G. ADMINISTRATIVE ENFORCEMENT
      Sec.i443.301.iiENFORCEMENT BY DEPARTMENT. (a) The department
 shall receive and investigate complaints concerning violations of this chapter by:
           (1)iia license holder under Subchapter C; or
           (2)iia registrant under Section 443.2025.
      (b)iiThe department may revoke, suspend, or refuse to renew a license or
 registration for a violation of this chapter or a rule adopted under this chapter.
      (c)iiThe department may impose an administrative penalty in an amount not
 to exceed $10,000 against a license holder or registrant for each violation of this
 chapter or a rule adopted under this chapter.
      (d)iiA proceeding under this section is a contested case under Chapter 2001,
 Government Code.
      SECTIONi18.iiThe following provisions of the Health and Safety Code are
 repealed:
           (1)iiSection 443.151(e);
           (2)iiSection 443.201; and
           (3)iiSections 443.202(a) and 443.2025(a) and (c).
       SECTIONi19.iiNot later than December 1, 2025, the executive
 commissioner of the Health and Human Services Commission shall adopt the
 rules required by Section 443.2026, Health and Safety Code, as added by this
 Act.
      SECTIONi20.ii(a) Except as otherwise provided by Subsection (b) of this
 section, the changes in law made by this Act apply to the manufacture, sale,
 delivery, or possession of a consumable hemp product that occurs on or after the
 effective date of this Act. The manufacture, sale, delivery, or possession of a
 consumable hemp product that occurs before the effective date of this Act is
 governed by the law in effect on the date the manufacture, sale, delivery, or
 possession occurred and the former law is continued in effect for that purpose.
      (b)iiA person selling consumable hemp products on the effective date of this
 Act is not required to register a product under Section 443.2026, Health and
 Safety Code, as added by this Act, before January 1, 2026.
      SECTIONi21.iiThis Act takes effect September 1, 2025.

 No.i2:

                    SUBCHAPTER A-1. NONAPPLICABILITY
       Sec.i443.021.iiLOW-THC CANNABIS. This chapter does not apply to

 hemp conducted by:

           (2)iia hospital licensed under Chapter 241; or
           (3)iian institution of higher education or private or independent
 institution of higher education, as defined by Section 61.003, Education Code.
